Blue Team Report

Team/Subgroup

Group Name: COPPOLA

NAME email testbed handle (original/war)
Sunita Verma (LEADER) sverma@cs cherry, catcpy
Madhavan Kasthurirangan madhavan@cs jingobingo, greyhound
Amit Bhosle bhosle@cs intruder, vetaal
Vishal Kher vkher@cs mutin, autocad
Doli Bambhania doli@cs dolibee, savior

Mission

Our mission was to detect intrusion on coppola and hide the flag. To this end, we had several responsibilities. First, we had to protect secret.txt mounted on host coppola. To do this, we decided to put the real secret.txt very deep in directory hierarchy (~3000 levels) and put a multiple copies of fake secret.txt all over the place. We had to monitor the activities on the host itself and detecting any unauthorized access / malicious activity. For this, we monitored lots of logs, used IP chains, TCP wrappers, Tripwire, and some UNIX commands like lsof, who, netstat, strace, etc.

RESPONSIBILITIES:

  • Sunita/Madhavan: Learning, configuring rules for IP chains and TCP wrappers and monitoring/maintaining respective logs during the exercise.
  • Amit/Vishal: Writing scripts to embed secret.txt deep, putting other fake secret.txt's, installing and configuring Snort.
  • Doli: Installing and configuring Tripwire, and monitoring activities during the war using it, helping out with Snort.

Preparation

  • Logging and protecting access to the host (IP chains and TCP Wrappers) - Madhavan /Sunita
  • Detecting any backdoors/ modification (Tripwire) - Doli
  • Changing the root login and password - This was everyone's suggestion and everyone modified the password file as necessary during the exercise.
  • Protecting the secret.txt file and logging any access to it (Perl script) and creating multiple fake copies of it - Amit/Vishal
  • Hiding the file deep in to OS. But the file was still accessible (access to the file was not denied) (perl script) - Amit/Vishal
  • Monitored whether the directory in which the file was kept was ever accessed and the file itself was ever accessed and wrote script generate logs
  • Tried to figure out to run snort on coppola. During the final day network group was executing snort hence we did not run it on coppola to avoid redundancy of logs - Amit/Vishal/Doli
  • Tried to write a program which can log all keystrokes of the user on a terminal (sort of Terminate and stay resident, TSR in DOS) but no such script was available for linux neither could we find enough information on net. - Amit/Vishal
  • Changed the name of root to pappu
  • We had even thought of the idea of creating a .profile with "exit" in it to serve as an auto-logout for the honeypot accounts. But Andrew had already created 'fush' for this purpose.

Execution

During the exercise, we all pretty much worked together to detect intrusion on the network. The tools that were used were logs created by TCP wrappers, IP Chains, Tripwire, in addition to using commands like 'w', 'netstat', 'who', 'lsof', 'strace', etc. It's difficult to give logs of everyone because often, we all discovered things simultaneously, so we'll give a log of what happened. (The times given are approximate)

TIME ACTIVITY
1:10 Detected pingscan from 128.111.48.41
1:12 Detected pingscan from 128.111.18.19
1:15 Detected scan from 128.111.48.41
1:20 removed user 'root', replaced it with 'pappu' and difficult to guess password
1:35 ftp-scan from packard.cs, bullwinkle.cs
1:35 attempt to log in as root by some 'outsiders' - not a member of our team
1:40 attempted login from audit5.cs.ucsb.edu (128.111.48.19)
1:47 attempt to telnet into coppola from packard.cs (128.111.48.41)
1:55 ftp attempt from packard.cs
2:10 attempt to crack root password from packard.cs
2:15 packard logged out
2:15 scan from 128.111.48.41 (packard.cs)
2:15
  • spoofing using 128.111.54.157 (compromised host ?)
  • attempt to rlogin
  • scan through password file
  • more rlogin attempts
  • more scans through password file
2:25
  • user 'zhoupin' compromised
  • intruder logged in as 'zhoupin' and logged out - connection from 128.111.43.36
  • connection accepted from 128.111.43.36
  • performed another scan through the password file
  • two outside hosts logged into coppola:
    • packard.cs
    • 128.111.54.157 (guessing this was a compromised host)
  • These connections were using ports ranging from 1020 - 1023
  • an attempt to connect from 'coppola' to 'coppola'
2:30 another scan
2:35
  • 'gethostbyname' error: rpc.statd - ftp buffer overflow
  • someone coming in from 'mamet', a compromised host
  • connection accepted from 128.111.49.43
  • user 'bdr' logged in!!! (backdoor)
  • can now login as 'root' without password
2:40 We made account 'root' inaccessible
2:50
  • Intruder created accounts 'bdr', 'bd', 'bdu, 'bdu1'
  • upon detection, we gave them all 'fush' shells
2:50 root (pappu) password changed!! Scary!
3:00 THE FLAG STILL NOT ACCESSED!!! YAY! :)

For reference, here are the logs.

Evaluation

Here is what we think:
  • We did a reasonably good job of handling our responsibilities and were successful to a large extent in protecting our machine.
  • We worked together well and had a lot of fun!
  • A remarkable point is that only the coppola flag remained uncaptured and untraced !! Thanks to all of us.

Miscellaneous

A lot of things made sense looking at logs afterwards and talking to people. For instance, in the mail log, there were mails sent from root to ashish@cs and mariabar@cs. These were the password files. We found this out when we talked to Ashish later.

References

  1. IPChains - http://www.sans.org/infosecFAQ/firewall/blocking_ipchains.htm
  2. TCP Wrappers - ftp://ftp.porcupine.org/pub/security/index.html#software
  3. Man pages for ipchains
  4. Man pages of lsof, fstat, perl, snort
  5. Tripwire download and documentation: http://www.tripwire.org
